5.Day 3: Resuspend and split cells from multi-layer flasks.CRITICAL: This day requires extensive cell culture work in a Cat II hood. Prepare all medium in advance and plan for gradual flask splitting with scheduled breaks.a.Pour lentiviral medium into a waste flask with 2% v/v Virkon or Vernagel.b.Wash each flask twice with 25 mL DPBS containing 1 mM EDTA and 0.2% v/v BSA.c.Add 20 mL of pre-warmed 20% v/v trypsin, spreading it evenly across the layers and incubate at 37°C for 5 min.d.Once the cells detach, add 30 mL of medium, mix, and pour the cell suspension into a 1 L bottle medium.e.Wash the flask with another 50 mL medium and pour into the same bottle.f.Repeat for all flasks, yielding 700 mL of cell suspension.g.Without counting cells, replate as follows:i.21 x T875 multi-layer flasks: 32.4 mL per flask; final volume 150 mL each.ii.2 x T175 flasks: 6.4 mL per flask; final volume 30 mL each.iii.3 × 100 mm dishes: 2.2 mL per dish; final volume 10 mL each. Use two dishes for BFP assessment and one as a control.Note: At this stage, cells have been infected for only 24 h. Residual lentivirus particles still present a biosafety risk, making it unsafe to handle the preparation outside the biosafety hood or dispense infected samples for cell counting. Distributing 700 mL of cell suspension across 21 × T875 flasks results in approximately 33 mL per flask. To accommodate cells for other formats, use 32.4 mL per T875 flask and reserve 12.6 mL for smaller formats: five times less for T175 flasks and fifteen times less for 100 mm dishes.Note: This is a high-throughput screen, so having at least a duplicate of each format provides a backup in case something goes wrong with one of them.CRITICAL: Once again, keep untransfected cells in T175 flasks or 150 mm dishes as controls for subsequent steps.

6.Day 4: Start puromycin selection.Note: Cells should be approximately 70% confluent.a.After 48 h post infection, add 300 μL of 4 mg/mL puromycin stock to each T875 multi-layer flask to achieve a final concentration of 8 μg/mL. Also, add puromycin to 2 x T175 flasks and 1 × 100 mm dish for controls.b.Incubate at 37°C.c.Replace the medium every 3 days for puromycin selection.

7.Day 6: Determine MOI by BFP expressiona.Take one 100 mm dish of untransduced cells and one of transduced but not puromycin-selected cells.b.Remove the medium, wash twice with 1 mL of DPBS and add 3 mL of 4 mM EDTA-DPBS.c.Collect cells with a 1 mL pipette, gently pipetting to break up clumps and transfer to a flow cytometry tube.d.Analyze BFP expression by flow cytometry.

Note: If the percentage of BFP-positive population is between 20%–40%, continue with the screen. If it is around 50%–60%, you can still proceed, but consider that there will be a higher likelihood of cells harboring more than one sgRNA during downstream validation.

Note: The safe handling of transduced cells outside of a BSL-II hood depends on the specific virus and institutional guidelines, which typically recommend waiting 72–96 h post transduction.

8.Day 7: Medium change and continue puromycin selection.a.Assess puromycin selection by examining the transduced plates under the microscope.b.Change the medium to remove dead cells and refresh puromycin.

Note: After 4 days of puromycin treatment, you should observe an increase in dying cells.

Note: Trypsinizing and re-plating the cells in puromycin on days 4-5 increases the selection of true puromycin-resistant cells.

9.Day 10: Splitting, expansion and first freeze of infected cells.CRITICAL: This is a long day of tissue culture (splitting 21 x T875 multi-layer flasks), so it is recommended to work with a colleague and share the workload. Aim to handle 7 flasks at a time.CRITICAL: In this step, keep the transduced cells in two or three pools (A, B and C) to reduce contamination risk. For practical reasons we chose to combine the gDNA extracted from the sorted pools to create a single gDNA sample and derivative library after each round of sorting. Alternatively, it is possible to keep the pools separate, generating separate libraries for each round of enrichment, but this significantly amplifies the workload.CRITICAL: Ensure each pool contains enough transduced cells for at least 300-400-fold library coverage. Keep at least 5 × 107 cells for freezing, gDNA extraction and cell expansion.a.Aspirate medium from the T875 flasks and wash twice with 25 mL of DPBS - 1 mM EDTA - 0.2% BSA.b.Add 20 mL of pre-warmed 20% trypsin, spreading it evenly across the layers. Incubate at 37°C for 5 min.c.Once the cells detach, add 30 mL of medium, distribute properly and pour the 50 mL of cell suspension into a 1 L bottle medium.d.Wash each flask with 50 mL medium and pour it into the same bottle.e.Repeat for all 21 flasks resulting in 2,100 mL cell suspension (100 mL/flask).f.Take a small sample to calculate the percentage of BFP-positive cells via flow cytometry (Figure 4C).CRITICAL: A BFP positivity rate above 85% indicates successful puromycin selection, justifying progression to the next step. If this percentage has not been reached, continue with puromycin selection before moving forward.g.Count cells from each pool.h.Cryopreserve live cells from each pool: Cryopreserve at least 5 × 107 transduced cells in cryovials to ensure backup in case of contamination. Use a density of 1–5 × 106 cells/mL in the freezing medium, or up to 2 × 107 cells/mL for large-scale experiments.i.Freeze cellular pellets for gDNA extraction from each pool: Freeze at least 2 × 15 mL tubes with 5 × 107 cells each per pool. Since this protocol involves extracting gDNA from fixed sorted cells, the transduced-unsorted cells must be fixed in 1% formaldehyde for 10 min before freezing to match the conditions of sorted cells.i.Transfer 5 × 107 cells into each of 2 × 15 mL conical tubes.ii.Centrifuge at 135 ×g for 5 min at 20°C–25°C.iii.Aspirate the medium, wash with 10 mL DPBS, centrifuge again at 135 ×g for 5 min and remove supernatant.iv.Resuspend cellular pellets in 10 mL DPBS.v.Add 270.2 μL of 37% formaldehyde to fix the cells.vi.Incubate for 10 min with orbital rotation at 20°C–25°C.vii.Centrifuge at 135 ×g for 5 min.viii.Wash pellets with 10 mL DPBS and carefully aspirate the supernatant.ix.Store pellets at −80°C until gDNA extraction.j.Expand cells from each pool: Maintain at least 5 × 107 cells/pool for expansion and future screening steps. Plate 3.5 × 106 cells per T175 flask across 15 flasks to ensure 400-fold library coverage.Note: Calculate the required number of cells for plating to ensure adequate representation across all treatments, considering the expected percentage of sorted cells (e.g., use an Excel spreadsheet to account for the initial number of cells plated, cells doubling time and seeding format).Note: Avoid discarding cells; freeze backups and samples for gDNA extraction after each splitting.

Cryopreserve live cells from each pool: Cryopreserve at least 5 × 107 transduced cells in cryovials to ensure backup in case of contamination. Use a density of 1–5 × 106 cells/mL in the freezing medium, or up to 2 × 107 cells/mL for large-scale experiments.

i.Freeze cellular pellets for gDNA extraction from each pool: Freeze at least 2 × 15 mL tubes with 5 × 107 cells each per pool. Since this protocol involves extracting gDNA from fixed sorted cells, the transduced-unsorted cells must be fixed in 1% formaldehyde for 10 min before freezing to match the conditions of sorted cells.i.Transfer 5 × 107 cells into each of 2 × 15 mL conical tubes.ii.Centrifuge at 135 ×g for 5 min at 20°C–25°C.iii.Aspirate the medium, wash with 10 mL DPBS, centrifuge again at 135 ×g for 5 min and remove supernatant.iv.Resuspend cellular pellets in 10 mL DPBS.v.Add 270.2 μL of 37% formaldehyde to fix the cells.vi.Incubate for 10 min with orbital rotation at 20°C–25°C.vii.Centrifuge at 135 ×g for 5 min.viii.Wash pellets with 10 mL DPBS and carefully aspirate the supernatant.ix.Store pellets at −80°C until gDNA extraction.

j.Expand cells from each pool: Maintain at least 5 × 107 cells/pool for expansion and future screening steps. 10.Day 13: Seed cells for phenotypic selection.Once transduced cells are over 85% BFP-positive or fully selected for puromycin resistance and expanded, plate them to induce the desired phenotype: in this case, increased intracellular polymer formation after CRISPR-Cas9 mutagenesis, detected by Mab2C1 staining (Figure 5).a.Aspirate the medium and wash each T175 flask with 10 mL of DPBS containing 1 mM EDTA and 0.2% BSA.b.Add 4 mL pre-warmed 20% trypsin, distribute and incubate at 37°C.c.Add 12 mL of CHO Ham’s F12 complete medium to neutralize the trypsin and detach the cells by pipetting gently.d.Transfer the suspension to low-binding polystyrene 250 mL bottles.e.Count cells for each pool (pool A and B) and the control cells, then seed as follows:i.Samples plates: For each pool, plate approximately 15 × 150 mm plates with 4.5 × 106 cells, totaling 6.75 × 107 cells (∼540-fold coverage) per pool; 30 plates to be treated with doxycycline.ii.Untreated control plate: 1 × 150 mm plate with 4.5 × 106 transduced cells (reference plate).iii.Unstained background control plate: 1 × 150 mm plate with 4.5 × 106 transduced cells that will be induced with doxycycline but not stained.iv.Uninfected untreated control plate: 1 × 150 mm plate with 4.5 × 106 uninfected cells untreated.v.Uninfected treated control plate: 1 × 150 mm plate with 4.5 × 106 uninfected cells treated.Note: To reduce the number of plates, consider seeding more cells per 150 mm plate than indicated in the cell seeding table from the cell line characterization section. In total, seed 34 × 150 mm plates: 15 plates for each pool (pool A and B) plus 4 control plates.

11.Day 14: Induce α1AT polymer expression with doxycycline.a.Prepare 1,000 mL of CHO Ham’s F12 complete medium (without puromycin) with 10 ng/mL doxycycline (add 100 μL of 100 μg/mL doxycycline stock).b.Aspirate medium from the sample plates, uninfected control plate and background control plate. Wash once with DPBS, and add 30 mL of doxycycline medium per plate.c.Change the medium for the untreated plates as well.d.Incubate at 37°C for 18 h.

Wash once with DPBS, and add 30 mL of doxycycline medium per plate.c.Change the medium for the untreated plates as well.d.Incubate at 37°C for 18 h.

12.Day 15: Large-scale cellular fixation.This protocol is designed to target an intracellular protein within the lumen of the endoplasmic reticulum, requiring membrane permeabilization using non-ionic detergents such as Triton X-100. To preserve cellular integrity, fixation must be performed prior to the permeabilization step.a.Aspirate the medium and wash twice with 10 mL of pre-warmed DPBS containing 1 mM EDTA and 0.2% BSA.b.Add 6 mL of pre-warmed DPBS containing 4 mM EDTA and 0.2% BSA.c.Incubate for 5–10 min at 37°C until single cells are visible.d.Transfer to several 50 mL tubes.e.Wash dishes with another 6 mL DPBS containing 4 mM EDTA and 0.2% BSA.f.Combine the cell suspension in the same collection tubes (12 mL/dish). Repeat for 34 dishes.Note: 50 mL collection tubes are used for practical reasons, as the cell strainer used in the next step, fits perfectly in these tubes.g.Pipette up and down with 1 mL pipette to achieve a single-cell suspension, at least 20 times per tube.h.Take a 10 μL sample to determine cell number and ensure they are single cells.i.Centrifuge at 135 ×g or 10 min at 20°C–25°C.j.Carefully aspirate part of the supernatant, retaining approximately 5 mL per tube and resuspend by pipetting up and down with a 1 mL pipette.k.Combine the cells and distribute into 50 mL tubes at 2–5 × 106 cells/mL.l.Filter through a 40 μm nylon mesh cell strainer, washing the filter with 5 mL of DPBS- 4 mM EDTA-0.2% BSA.m.Take another 10 μL sample to check cell number, single cells status, and cell loss.Note: Our empirical optimization suggests that cell loss during filtration can range from 10%–30%. If needed, continue pipette until single cells are visible.n.Fix cells with 1% formaldehyde by directly adding 1/36 volume of 37% formaldehyde to the tubes and rotate for 10 min at 20°C–25°C.o.Centrifuge at 184 ×g for 10 min at 20°C–25°C.p.Discard the supernatant and resuspend in 5 mL of DPBS-4 mM EDTA-0.2% BSA.q.Transfer the cell suspension to 5 mL low-binding protein tubes.r.Incubate at 4°C without rotation for staining the next day.

Day 15: Large-scale cellular fixation.

13.Day 16: Large-scale permeabilization and intracellular polymer staining for FACS analysis.CRITICAL: Permeabilization and fixation increase cell stickiness and the formation of cell clumps (so-called doublets), reducing sorting efficiency. To mitigate this, include EDTA and BSA in the sorting buffer, maintain cell concentrations between 1–10 × 10⁶, use a 1 mL pipette for pipetting in all steps and check cells under a microscope with a coverslip.Note: To shorten the protocol, combine permeabilization and blocking into a single step using a permeabilization/blocking buffer (DPBS containing 0.1% Triton X-100 and 10% FBS).Note: Use 5 mL low-binding protein tubes for staining, sorting and pellet visualization to reduce cell loss during washes.a.Centrifuge cells at 685 ×g, 5 min at 20°C–25°C.b.Aspirate the supernatant and resuspend the cells in 5 mL of permeabilization/blocking buffer. Gently pipette and incubate with orbital rotation for 30 min at 20°C–25°C.c.Centrifuge at 685 ×g for 5 min and discard the supernatant.d.Resuspend the cells in permeabilization/blocking buffer containing the primary antibody (Mab2C1; 1 μg/mL). For controls, use 350 μL of 1 μg/mL antibody solution; for sorting samples, use 1,125 μL of 1 μg/mL antibody solution. Incubate for 45 min with orbital rotation at 20°C–25°C.Note: For FACS staining, typically use 1–10 μg of antibody per million cells. For example, use 1–10 μg of primary antibody in a total volume of 100 μL to 1 mL. Mab2C1 is used at 1ug/mL; but other antibodies may require titration.e.Wash the cells 3 times with 5 mL of permeabilization/blocking buffer by centrifugation at 685 ×g for 5 min. Resuspend the pellet gently with a 1 mL pipette after each wash.f.Prepare secondary DyLight 633 Ab (1:1,000) in permeabilization/blocking buffer. Use 350 μL for controls and 1,125 μL for samples, both at the same concentration. Incubate for 30 min with orbital rotation at 20°C–25°C in the dark.Note: Secondary antibodies for FACS staining are generally diluted from 1:100 to 1:1,000. Optimize the dilution through titration for the best signal and minimal background.g.Wash cells 3 times with 5 mL of permeabilization/blocking buffer by centrifugation at 685 ×g for 5 min. Resuspend the pellet carefully after each wash.h.Resuspend the cells in DPBS containing 2 mM EDTA and 2% BSA. Keep them in the dark at 4°C with gentle agitation.i.For sorting purposes, concentrated samples are preferable. Reduce the cell suspension volume by half by gentle centrifugation at 685 ×g for 5 min at 20°C–25°C and resuspend. Keep in the dark at 4°C with gentle agitation overnight until the next step.

CRITICAL: Permeabilization and fixation increase cell stickiness and the formation of cell clumps (so-called doublets), reducing sorting efficiency. To mitigate this, include EDTA and BSA in the sorting buffer, maintain cell concentrations between 1–10 × 10⁶, use a 1 mL pipette for pipetting in all steps and check cells under a microscope with a coverslip.

Note: To shorten the protocol, combine permeabilization and blocking into a single step using a permeabilization/blocking buffer (DPBS containing 0.1% Triton X-100 and 10% FBS).

Note: Use 5 mL low-binding protein tubes for staining, sorting and pellet visualization to reduce cell loss during washes.

Incubate for 30 min with orbital rotation at 20°C–25°C in the dark.Note: Secondary antibodies for FACS staining are generally diluted from 1:100 to 1:1,000. Optimize the dilution through titration for the best signal and minimal background.

Prepare secondary DyLight 633 Ab (1:1,000) in permeabilization/blocking buffer. Use 350 μL for controls and 1,125 μL for samples, both at the same concentration. Incubate for 30 min with orbital rotation at 20°C–25°C in the dark.Note: Secondary antibodies for FACS staining are generally diluted from 1:100 to 1:1,000. Optimize the dilution through titration for the best signal and minimal background.

Note: Secondary antibodies for FACS staining are generally diluted from 1:100 to 1:1,000. Optimize the dilution through titration for the best signal and minimal background.

g.Wash cells 3 times with 5 mL of permeabilization/blocking buffer by centrifugation at 685 ×g for 5 min. Resuspend the pellet carefully after each wash.

Wash cells 3 times with 5 mL of permeabilization/blocking buffer by centrifugation at 685 ×g for 5 min. Resuspend the pellet carefully after each wash.

h.Resuspend the cells in DPBS containing 2 mM EDTA and 2% BSA. Keep them in the dark at 4°C with gentle agitation.

Resuspend the cells in DPBS containing 2 mM EDTA and 2% BSA. Keep them in the dark at 4°C with gentle agitation.

i.For sorting purposes, concentrated samples are preferable. Reduce the cell suspension volume by half by gentle centrifugation at 685 ×g for 5 min at 20°C–25°C and resuspend. Keep in the dark at 4°C with gentle agitation overnight until the next step.

For sorting purposes, concentrated samples are preferable. Reduce the cell suspension volume by half by gentle centrifugation at 685 ×g for 5 min at 20°C–25°C and resuspend. Keep in the dark at 4°C with gentle agitation overnight until the next step.

14.Day 17: FACS Sorting of CRISPR-mutagenized fixed cells.This protocol describes FACS using a Beckman Coulter MoFlo cell sorter, typically operated in a flow cytometry core facility. Parameters may need to be optimized by the investigator, especially if a different flow cytometer is used.a.Keep the following numbers in mind for the sorting day:i.Sorting capacity: Typically, 3 × 107 cells/h.ii.Initial sorting amount: For the first enrichment round, collect around 2% of the total sorted cells with the highest staining signal to enrich for the strongest phenotypes.iii.Number of cells to sort for 24-fold library representation: 15 × 107 cells.Numberofcellstosort=(librarysizexcoverage)/(%sortedcells/100)15x107=(125,000x24)/(2/100)iv.Number of cells to collect: 3 × 106 cells.Numberofcellstocollect=librarysizexcoverage3,000,000=125,000x24Note: Keep the cells on ice and cover them with foil to protect from light.Note: Sorting 3 million cells takes at least 5 h. However, low efficiency due to doublets can slow the process; allocate a full day to ensure sufficient recovery.b.Prepare cells for sorting:i.From step “13.i”, gently pipette each sample with a 1 mL pipette after keeping it overnight at 4°C to ensure single cells.ii.Take a 10 μL sample to verify the presence of single cells.iii.Filter the cell suspension from both pools (A and B) through a 40 μm cell strainer, collecting the filtered suspension in 50 mL tubes.iv.Transfer the filtered cells to 5 mL polypropylene round-bottom tubes, commonly used for cell sorting, and keep on ice. The cells are ready for sorting.v.Prepare 5 mL low-binding tubes with 500 μL CHO Ham’s F12 complete medium to collect 3–5 million sorted cells per pool.Note: Before sorting samples en masse, we recommend analyzing controls and a few samples by flow cytometry to determine the percentage of cells with the phenotype of interest and to design the gating strategy.Note: If low sorting efficiency is observed, refer to Troubleshooting 2.c.Set gates for sorting:i.FSC-A vs. SSC-A for live cells.ii.FSC-W vs. SSC to identify single cells and exclude doublets.iii.The fluorescent channels will depend on your strategy (e.g., Mab2C1 stained cells were detected with an excitation laser at 640 nm and a 670/14 nm emission filter).Note: Begin by running unstained samples and controls to establish appropriate gates. Sort pools A and B. The fixation process outlined in this protocol introduces convenient stopping points; if sorting both pools in one day is not possible, store the second pool in the dark at 4°C and sort it the following day.Note: Refer to Troubleshooting 3 if the BFP signal is faint during sorting.d.Keep sorted cells tubes on ice for immediate gDNA extraction.

Extraction of gDNA from sorted fixed cells

Timing: 3 days Timing: 1 hourfor step 15 Timing: 2 daysfor step 16 15.Day 17: Start extracting genomic DNA (gDNA) from sorted fixed cells.Fixation and permeabilization may increase the transparency of the cells, rendering the pellet from 3 million sorted cells difficult to visualize. It is essential to proceed with caution, ensuring thorough washing of the sorting collection tube walls with sorting collection buffer to recover all cells. Refer to Troubleshooting 4 for additional guidance.a.Centrifuge the sorted cells from both pools at 376 ×g at 4°C for 20 min.b.Transfer the supernatant to a clean 15 mL tube and store at 4°C as a backup until the gDNA yield result is determined.c.Resuspend the cellular pellet in 6 mL of gDNA proteinase K (PK) lysis buffer. Incubate in a thermomixer at 300 rpm at 50°C for 16 h.Note: The extraction of gDNA from transduced-unsorted-fixed cells will occur following the creation of the second derivative sgRNA library during library preparation for next-generation sequencing (NGS).16.Days 18 and 19: Continue gDNA extraction from sorted fixed cells.a.On the following day, reverse formaldehyde crosslinks by increasing the NaCl concentration to 500 mM. Incubate at 50°C–60°C for an additional 16–24 h.b.Precipitate the gDNA by adding an equal volume of isopropanol (6 mL). Mix gently by inversion.c.Incubate at −20°C for 16 h to facilitate gDNA precipitation.d.Next day, centrifuge at 4000 ×g for 15 min at 4°C to pellet the gDNA.e.Discard the supernatant, retaining approximately 1 mL.f.Resuspend the pellet in the remaining 1 mL and transfer to a 1.5 mL low-binding tube.g.Centrifuge at 21,000 x g for 15 min at 4°C, remove the supernatant and wash with 1 mL of cold 70% ethanol.h.Repeat the centrifugation and remove the ethanol.i.Air-dry pellet until it appears slightly translucent.j.Resuspend the pellet in 150–250 μL of 10 mM Tris pH 8 or nuclease-free water.k.Incubate at 55°C–65°C for 30–60 min to ensure complete dissolution.Note: For larger gDNA pellets, such as those from transduced-unsorted cells, this step may require several hours.Note: To enhance subsequent PCR results, sheer DNA using a 29G needle.l.Combine gDNA extracted from both pools (A and B) to create a single gDNA sample for sgRNA amplification and the creation of an enriched derivative sgRNA library.Note: Remember, this step is performed for practical reasons, allowing the generation of a single enriched sgRNA library following each round of sorting.m.Measure the gDNA concentration by nanodrop.n.Store the gDNA at −20°C or −80°C until further use.Note: From 3–5 × 106 sorted cells, expect to get 16–27 μg of gDNA, assuming 5.4 pg DNA per cell.19

PCR amplification and cloning of integrated sgRNAs to create derivative libraries enriched in relevant guides

Timing: Approximately 2 weeks Timing: 1 weekfor step 17 17.Day 20: Start with PCR amplification of integrated sgRNAs to create enriched libraries.The purpose of this PCR is to amplify the sgRNA region from gDNA isolated from the fixed sorted population, to create enriched CRISPR libraries for screening (Figure 6). The amplified 382 bp DNA fragment will be ligated into the parental CRISPR lentiviral backbone, resulting in production of libraries enriched in the desired phenotype producing sgRNAs to be used for further rounds of selection or analysis.CRITICAL: Use all recovered gDNA for amplification to ensure adequate library coverage. Use Hot Start High-Fidelity DNA Polymerase for the PCR.a.Design primers that amplify the sgRNA region while keep restriction sites that facilitate subsequent cloning into the CRISPR backbone plasmid (pKLV-U6gRNA(BbsI)-PGKpuro2ABFP; Addgene#50946).18 The PCR product will thus represent a pool of sgRNAs targeting the desired genes. For this CHO-K1 CRISPR KO sgRNA library, use the following set of primers:Cloning PCR: Primers for amplifying integrated sgRNAs to generate enriched derivative librariesForward primer: Pr2182_sgRNA_outer_MluI_short_FCAGCAGAGATCCAGTTTGGTTAGTACCReserve primer: Pr1758_sgRNA_outer_shortGAATGTGTGCGAGGCCAGAGExpected size of the PCR product: 535 bpNote: These primers keep MluI and BamHI restriction sites for cloning into the lentiviral backbone. The design may vary by library.CRITICAL: Verify that sgRNA sequences are compatible with the chosen restriction enzymes. Utilizing MluI and BamHI minimizes sgRNA loss, retaining approximately 99% of the CHO-K1 sgRNA library.b.Prepare a PCR mix according to the table below, where “x” is the volume of gDNA (for optimal yield, use 2–3 μg of DNA per reaction). 17.Day 20: Start with PCR amplification of integrated sgRNAs to create enriched libraries.The purpose of this PCR is to amplify the sgRNA region from gDNA isolated from the fixed sorted population, to create enriched CRISPR libraries for screening (Figure 6). Day 20: Start with PCR amplification of integrated sgRNAs to create enriched libraries.

The purpose of this PCR is to amplify the sgRNA region from gDNA isolated from the fixed sorted population, to create enriched CRISPR libraries for screening (Figure 6). The amplified 382 bp DNA fragment will be ligated into the parental CRISPR lentiviral backbone, resulting in production of libraries enriched in the desired phenotype producing sgRNAs to be used for further rounds of selection or analysis.CRITICAL: Use all recovered gDNA for amplification to ensure adequate library coverage. The formula to calculate the required insert is as follows:ngofinsert=ratiox[lengthofinsert(bp)/lengthofvector(bp)]xngofvectorFor example, for 50 ng of a 7.7 kb vector and a 385 bp insert at a 1:3 ratio, approximately 7 ng of insert is required: [3 x (383/7700) x 50].Note: When using highly-efficient cells (108 CFU/μg DNA), using 50 ng of vector and 7.4 ng of insert should yield approximately 5.7 million CFU. The total number of transformants (CFU) should be 10–20 times the size of the library pool to ensure adequate representation.10 A single ligation is sufficient to maintain sgRNA diversity (5.7 million / 125,000 = 45.6).CFU=TransformationEfficiency×[TotalDNA(ng)/1000(ng)]b.Incubate the reaction at 16°C in a PCR thermoblock for 16–18 h.

High-scale transformation and electroporation

Timing: 5 days Note: Prepare SOC medium, agar plates, miniprep tubes, 50 mL tubes and electroMAX Stbl4 competent cells. Note: For the expansion of the sgRNA full library, ensure that the total number of transformants is 10 to 20 times greater that the library pool size to maintain adequate representation (e.g., 1.25–2.5 × 106 transformants for initial expansion). Note: For derivative libraries, anticipate a decrease in library complexity, dependent on the experimental approach. For this reason, for the first round of enrichment, it is recommended to aim for a minimum of 1.25 × 106 transformants. Note: The maximum number of transformants/agar plate suggested is approximately 20,000 on 100 mm plates and between 60,000 and 100,000 on 150 mm plates. This is greater than the theoretical limit for 150 mm plates (60,000-100,000) for separate colony formation but is used for practical reasons to reduce the number of plates required. CRITICAL: Prior to scaling up, conduct small-scale transformations using chemically competent Stbl2 cells. While both Stbl2 and Stbl4 cells are designed for enhanced plasmid stability, electroMAX Stbl4 cells generally incur a higher cost due to their improved capabilities and potentially higher transformation efficiency. Therefore, Stbl2 cells, which are more affordable, can be used for small-scale transformations to establish initial conditions using the manufacturer’s instructions. 20.Day 29: Prepare high-scale library transformation:a.Warm 3 × 100 mm and 13 × 150 mm LB (Luria-Bertani) agar plates containing 100 μg/mL ampicillin plates in an incubator at 37°C for 30 min.b.Place the ligation reaction on ice.c.Thaw 1 vial of 100 μL of electroMAX Stbl4 competent cells on ice.d.Add 5 μL of the ligation reaction to the 100 μL Stbl4 cells and gently flick the tube.e.Distribute 21 μL of the ligation-Stbl4 competent reaction mixture into 5 pre-chilled pulser electroporation cuvettes, tapping gently to ensure all components are settled at the bottom.f.Electroporate using the following settings: Voltage: 1,200 V; Capacitance: 25 μF; Resistance: 200 Ω; Cuvette: 1 mm.g.Immediately after the pulse, add 1 mL of SOC medium to each cuvette and transfer the mixture to a 50 mL tube.h.Rinse each cuvette with an additional 1 mL of SOC medium.i.Repeat the electroporation and pooling process for all 5 cuvettes, totaling 10 mL.j.Shake the pooled volume at 30°C, 225 rpm for 90 min.k.Plate 1 mL on each of the 150 mm LB-amp plates (10 in total); reserve 25 μL for titration.Note: Allow the agar plates to dry on the bench until all the bacterial suspension is fully absorbed.l.Perform titration on 100 mm LB-ampicillin plates using 1/10, 1/100, and 1/1,000 dilutions:i.1/10: Mix 225 μL SOC + 25 μL electroporation mix, then plate 100 μL.ii.1/100: Mix 25 μL from 1/10 dilution + 225 μL SOC, then plate 100 μL.iii.1/1000: Mix 25 μL from 1/100 dilution + 225 μL SOC, then plate 100 μL.m.Incubate plates at 30°C for 3 days until a bacterial lawn forms.n.Count colonies on titration plates and scale up to calculate the total number of transformants on the 150 mm plates.o.Harvest bacterial lawn by adding 5 mL LB medium and lifting the colonies with a L-shaped cell spreader making a suspension.p.Transfer the suspension to a 250 mL bottle; rinse plates with another 5 mL LB medium.q.Pool the bacterial suspensions from 10 × 150 mm plates.r.Centrifuge at 1503 x g for 30 min at 4°C.s.Discard supernatant and calculate the pellet weight by comparing the pellet containing bottle to an empty 250 mL bottle.t.Extract DNA using the QIAGEN Plasmid maxiprep kit (0.5–1 g per maxiprep) following the manufacturer’s instructions: https://www.qiagen.com/us/resources/resourcedetail?id=0bd0c5fb-c271-43e7-af43-32d539374fa9&lang=enu.Measure gDNA concentration using a nanodrop.Note: Validating the diversity of your sgRNA library through Sanger sequencing is optional but recommended. This step ensures the library's complexity by confirming balanced representation of each base in the guide region (Figure 8) prior to downstream applications.v.Store the newly derived enriched library plasmid at −20°C until further use.

Iterative CRISPR screens with derivative enriched CRISPR libraries

Timing: 2 months Note: Approximately 2 months for 2 rounds of iterative enrichment with 2 libraries. 21.Following the generation of the first derivative enriched library, repeat the entire procedure from lentiviral production through to infection of parental cells, puromycin selection, phenotype induction, polymer staining in fixed cells and sorting to PCRs.22.Create two iterative libraries to enhance the population exhibiting the desired phenotype by repeating the entire process twice. Note: Derivative libraries will experience a reduction in complexity. While it is challenging to precisely estimate the extent of this reduction, elimination of sgRNAs targeting essential genes (approximately 1,000-2,000 genes in the human genome)10,21 may account for at least 10% reduction in complexity. Additionally, another 20% reduction may occur during the selection process, leading to an overall estimated complexity reduction of about 30% in the first round. The table below provides guidance for planning successive enrichment rounds: Estimated numbersLib0Lib1 enriched libraryLib2 enriched libraryLibrary complexity125,030∼80,000-90,000∗∼56,000-63,000∗T175 flasks for virus production621Cells to infect2.1 × 1088 × 1075 × 107Plating formatT875 flasksT175 flasksT175 flasksSorted cells3–5 × 1063–5 × 1063–5 × 106∗These numbers are estimations according with our experimental parameters.

PCRs amplification, barcode attachment, and library preparation for NGS

Timing: 2 weeks

Timing: 2 weeks

After two rounds of FACS enrichment, the aim of this step is to use the gDNA extracted from transduced sorted and unsorted samples to amplify sgRNA sequences and add adaptors/barcodes for NGS via two-step nested PCR (Figure 7).Note: Most CRISPR studies recommend having sufficient gDNA to cover 300-fold the full library complexity. For transduced unsorted cells (controls), assuming 5.4 pg DNA/cell,19 at least 202.2 μg DNA (125,000 × 300 × 5.4/1,000,000) should be extracted from 3.75 × 107 cells (125,000 × 300). For transduced sorted samples, the expected amount of DNA is 16–27 μg of DNA, derived from 3–5 × 106 sorted cells.CRITICAL: Use all recovered gDNA, up to or exceeding 300x the library size.CRITICAL: Conduct trial PCRs with appropriate controls and optimize the number of cycles (typically 18-22). Select the cycle that yields a visible PCR product without saturation. Also, run a 30-cycle PCR to confirm product presence.23.Create a table to outline the samples intended for NGS submission, as illustrated below:SampleSample descriptionSequencing information1Cells transduced with full library (Lib0)_Untreated and unsorted_Pool AProvides data on initial library complexity and loss of essential genes2Cells transduced with full library (Lib 0)_Untreated and unsorted_Pool BServes as a second independent replicate to confirm the initial sgRNA levels in unsorted cells3Cells transduced with derivative enriched Library 1 (Lib1)_Untreated and UnsortedIndicates reduction in library complexity in the first derivative library4Cells transduced with derivative enriched Library 1 (Lib1)_ Sorted_Bright for Mab2C1_Pool AProvides information on the most enriched sgRNAs associated with the observed phenotype5Cells transduced with derivative enriched Library 1 (Lib1)_ Sorted_Bright for Mab2C1_Pool BServes as a second independent replicate, providing information on the most enriched sgRNAs linked to the observed phenotype6Cells transduced with derivative enriched Library 2 (Lib2) Untreated and UnsortedShows the reduction in library complexity in the second derivative library7Cells transduced with derivative enriched Lib2_Sorted_Bright for Mab2C1_Pool AProvides data on the second round of enriched sgRNAs corresponding to the observed phenotype8Cells transduced with derivative enriched Lib2_Sorted_Bright for Mab2C1_Pool BServes as a second independent replicate, providing information on enriched sgRNAs in the second enrichment roundNote: This table lists the minimum number of samples to be submitted. If the researcher has sorted cells into different bins based on M2C1 brightness levels, additional samples may be included in the final library preparation for NGS submission. Expected size: 322 bp.h.Measure gDNA concentration using a nanodrop.i.Store the pooled PCR product at −20°C.Figure 7Schematic diagram of the universal two-step nested PCR approach for next generation sequencing (NGS)The first-round PCR employs sequence-specific primers (Pr1434 and Pr1758) to amplify the sgRNA sequence region, generating an amplicon suitable for second-round PCR. For sgRNA libraries, 18-22 cycles are recommended. The second-round PCR utilizes primers tagged with universal P5 and P7 linker sequences (Pr1432 and PrNEBNXT -P7 primers), which are complementary to the immobilized primers in the NGS Illumina flow cells. For sgRNA libraries, 6-14 cycles are recommended in the second-round PCR. For each sample, a different PrNEBNext -P7 primer will be used featuring unique barcodes to enable multiplex sequencing. This nested PCR approach enhances the specificity of amplification.

Note: Most CRISPR studies recommend having sufficient gDNA to cover 300-fold the full library complexity. For transduced unsorted cells (controls), assuming 5.4 pg DNA/cell,19 at least 202.2 μg DNA (125,000 × 300 × 5.4/1,000,000) should be extracted from 3.75 × 107 cells (125,000 × 300). For transduced sorted samples, the expected amount of DNA is 16–27 μg of DNA, derived from 3–5 × 106 sorted cells.

CRITICAL: Use all recovered gDNA, up to or exceeding 300x the library size.

CRITICAL: Conduct trial PCRs with appropriate controls and optimize the number of cycles (typically 18-22). Select the cycle that yields a visible PCR product without saturation. Also, run a 30-cycle PCR to confirm product presence.

25.Prepare PCR2 for NGSThis second PCR round aims to add the P5 and P7 NEB Next adaptors that attach DNA fragments to the sequencing flow cell. Schematic diagram of the universal two-step nested PCR approach for next generation sequencing (NGS)

The first-round PCR employs sequence-specific primers (Pr1434 and Pr1758) to amplify the sgRNA sequence region, generating an amplicon suitable for second-round PCR. For sgRNA libraries, 18-22 cycles are recommended. The second-round PCR utilizes primers tagged with universal P5 and P7 linker sequences (Pr1432 and PrNEBNXT -P7 primers), which are complementary to the immobilized primers in the NGS Illumina flow cells. For sgRNA libraries, 6-14 cycles are recommended in the second-round PCR. For each sample, a different PrNEBNext -P7 primer will be used featuring unique barcodes to enable multiplex sequencing. This nested PCR approach enhances the specificity of amplification.

Assess PCR quality and purity

Timing: 1 week

Timing: 1 week

Equal representation of samples is crucial for NGS multicomplex libraries. The step uses an Agilent 2100 Bioanalyzer for high-resolution nucleic acid analysis and quantification after generating PCR2 with Illumina adaptors. If contaminates are identified, refer to Troubleshooting 5.26.Prepare samples for Agilent DNA1000 Chip.a.Dilute each PCR2 product at ratios 1:2 and 1:5 in water. Analyze the samples using the Agilent DNA1000 Chip, following the manufacture’s protocol guidelines: https://www.agilent.com/cs/library/usermanuals/public/G2938-90014_DNA1000Assay_KG.pdf.Note: The DNA1000 chips require 1 μL of DNA sample at concentrations between 0.1 and 10 ng/μL. Based in our experience, dilutions of 1:2 or 1:5 typically yield to 2–5 ng/μL. Analyze each sample at two different concentrations to enhance quantification accuracy.b.Adjust all samples to the lowest concentration determined and re-run them on the chip.27.Pool equal volume of each sample to create a single NGS multicomplex sample, achieving a final volume of 15 μL. High-throughput sequencing and data analysis

Timing: 3 weeks

Timing: 3 weeks

The PCR-amplified sgRNA library undergo high-throughput sequencing to determine sgRNA abundance in the enriched populations using the PAGE purified primer Pr1435. In this protocol, we employed the HiSeq4000 platform, conducting single reads of 50 bp across 2 lanes, resulting in a total of 600 million reads.

Computational methods are used to identify sgRNAs that are significantly enriched in the phenotype of interest versus controls, therefore pinpointing genes or regulatory elements associated with the phenotype. A variety of tools have been developed to analyses NGS data from CRISPR screens. For instance, MAGeCK software15 can analyses sgRNA read counts and gene rankings, while Metascape16 can conduct gene ontology analyses.

Expected outcomes

This protocol expands the toolkit of CRISPR-based gene discovery, to instances in which phenotypic selection requires (or benefits from) cell fixation and permeabilization. This opens the door to screens based on sgRNA-induced changes in signal arising from immunostaining of an intracellular antigen. In addition, given the negative impact of phenotypic drift on the reliability of CRISPR-based screens that rely on transgene activity,5 the power of this protocol to minimize such drift expands its applicability to CRISPR screens with reporter-based on phenotypic selection.

This protocol is generalizable and can be adapted for genetically dissecting a broad range of mammalian processes using CRISPR-Cas9 screens. We anticipate significant interest from researchers engaged in somatic cell genetic approaches to address fundamental questions in cell biology. The protocol is also beneficial for those conducting whole-genome CRISPR-Cas9 screens, offering a streamlined and efficient method for the iterative enrichment of sgRNAs.

This protocol was developed to identify regulators of intracellular polymer levels of α1AT. The protocol achieved a modest enrichment of the target phenotype after two rounds of sorting with the enriched libraries (Figure 5). Notably, we identified a significant number of hits, including LMAN1, a well-characterized cargo receptor implicated in α1AT trafficking.1 Once the most promising hit genes are selected, those should undergo validation by the generation of single knockouts via CRISPR editing, ideally using two independent sgRNAs. In a successfully CRISPR screen, single knockouts should replicate the observed phenotype in the screen.

It is expected that the derivative libraries maintain library complexity, as evidenced by the equal representation of each base in the guide region determined by Sanger sequencing (Figure 8). Moreover, this protocol involves the extraction of gDNA from 1% formaldehyde-fixed cells, a concentration anticipated to yield visible and high-quality PCR products on 2% agarose gels following all PCR and cloning procedures. To prevent PCR over-amplification, it is advisable to select a number of cycles that produce a faint but specific band, which many vary among samples (Figure 9).Figure 8Sanger sequencing chromatograph confirming the complexity of derivative enriched librariesThe region labeled “20N” indicates genomic DNA (gDNA) where the analysis software has difficulty in assigning bases, causing the appearance of “Ns” in the sequence. This corresponds to the area where sgRNAs are integrated into the genome, reflecting the library’s diversity.Figure 9Analysis of PCR products by gel electrophoresis of gDNA extracted from fixed cellsExamples shown on 2% agarose-TBE gels.(A) PCR amplification using primers designed for cloning PCR, yielding a strong PCR amplicon band at ∼535 bp. In this case, 20 PCR cycles with a very faint band were recommended for the cloning PCR.(B) For the first round of PCR-next-generation sequencing (PCR1-NGS), a specific PCR1 amplicon band at ∼300 bp was generated, with 22 cycles recommended in this example.(C) Finally, 10 cycles were recommended for the PCR2-NGS using the primer combination (Pr1432 vs. Pr1760), resulting in a weak but correct PCR2 amplicon band at ∼300 bp. Asterisks (∗) denote an excess of primers. Red arrows show the selected number of cycles for each PCR step.

Analysis of PCR products by gel electrophoresis of gDNA extracted from fixed cells

Examples shown on 2% agarose-TBE gels.

(A) PCR amplification using primers designed for cloning PCR, yielding a strong PCR amplicon band at ∼535 bp. In this case, 20 PCR cycles with a very faint band were recommended for the cloning PCR.

(B) For the first round of PCR-next-generation sequencing (PCR1-NGS), a specific PCR1 amplicon band at ∼300 bp was generated, with 22 cycles recommended in this example.

(C) Finally, 10 cycles were recommended for the PCR2-NGS using the primer combination (Pr1432 vs. Pr1760), resulting in a weak but correct PCR2 amplicon band at ∼300 bp. Asterisks (∗) denote an excess of primers. Red arrows show the selected number of cycles for each PCR step.

Limitations

As practiced in Ordóñez et al., 2021,1 the use of restriction digests to prepare derivative libraries of sgRNAs that have been phenotypically enriched carries a small risk of losing sgRNAs containing the restriction sites within their insert sequences. However, since each gene is targeted by six independent sgRNAs, the likelihood of losing all sgRNAs for any single gene remains low. This limitation may be circumvented by employing restriction enzyme-free methods, such as PCR-Gibson assembly, to construct derivative libraries.

This approach relies on the effective recovery of sgRNA sequences from gDNA via PCR, and depends on fixation methods that preserve gDNA. Success also depends on preserving the representation of sgRNA species during PCR amplification and subsequent derivative library construction. This is attainable with attention to detail and the use of highly competent bacteria, and it represents a predictable and surmountable challenge.

Troubleshooting

Problem 1

Low titer of lentivirus production.

Potential solution

Low titers in lentivirus production are often associated with poor-quality producer cells. It is critical to work with low-passage cells and seed them at optimal density for transfection to enhance virus production. To resolve this problem, we advise thawing a fresh vial of low-passage cells and conducting small-scale virus production tests until achieving satisfactory titers. Additionally, ensure that the transfection reagents are not expired, as packaging vectors typically do not pose issues.

Problem 2

Inefficient sorting efficiency and low number of sorted cells.

Potential solution

Inefficient cell sorting results from poor sample quality and cell clumping. Cells often become stickier during permeabilization and fixation, increasing this risk. To improve sample quality and sorting efficiency, maintain a BSA concentration of 0.2%–2% in all buffers and use gentle pipetting with a 1 mL pipette throughout the process. Additionally, filter samples through a 40 μm nylon mesh before sorting. If many doublets are observed during sorting, re-filter the sample.

Problem 3

Absence of BFP signal during FACS sorting.

Potential solution

The BFP (Blue Fluorescent Protein) signal is often weaker than GFP (Green Fluorescent Protein) or mCherry signals in FACS analysis. This is because BFP is excited by violet or near-ultraviolet light (around 405 nm), which generally has lower excitation efficiency in many flow cytometers, resulting in a dimmer signal. Additionally, BFP tends to photobleaching more quickly than GFP or mCherry, which can further weaken the signal during extended or repeated excitation in prolonged FACS sorting. Therefore, despite the low BFP signal, sort cells based on the readout of your phenotype rather than the BFP signal itself. In this case, the readout was a bright signal for Mab2C1 staining. Additionally, cells are selected using puromycin resistance, ensuring that only transduced cells are chosen, even if the BFP signal is dim.

Problem 4

Absence of a conspicuous pellet from sorted cells.

Potential solution

Permeabilization results in increased transparency of cells after FACS. If no visible pellet is observed post-sorting, we recommend adding glycogen as a carrier molecule for nucleic acid precipitation to enhance confidence in the gDNA extraction process. Add glycogen at a final concentration of 0.1–0.4 μg/mL to facilitate visible pellet formation, which will not affect the spectrophotometric determination of nucleic acid concentration. Although, glycogen does not interfere with most downstream applications (PCR, DNA sequencing, ligation) we recommend using the minimum amount to yield a visible pellet. Follow the protocol provided for further guidance. http://www.bio-protech.com.tw/upload/20170919042645.pdf.

Problem 5

Presence of contaminants in the PCR products for NGS.

Potential solution

Addressing contamination issues in PCR products for NGS is critical for obtaining reliable sequencing data. Adhere to sterile pipetting techniques throughout the PCR setup process to prevent cross-contamination. Use filter barrier pipette tips, molecular-grade water and high-quality reagents to minimize the introduction of contaminants. Regularly perform quality control checks on reagents to ensure their integrity and purity. If contamination persists, clean the amplicon with the Agencourt AMPure XP PCR∗ purification kit, which uses magnetic beads to efficiently remove contaminants such as primers, dNTPs, salts and enzymes from PCR reactions, leaving behind purified DNA suitable for downstream applications such as sequencing, cloning, or genotyping (Figure 10).Figure 10PCR contamination troubleshooting and potential solutionsGel images depicting the quality control steps for PCR2-NGS amplicons.(A) Analysis of a gel image from a Agilent 2100 Bioanalyzer high sensitivity DNA Chip run reveals the presence of contaminants, denoted by asterisks.(B) Following purification with an AMPure XP PCR purification kit, the same samples were re-run and analyzed, demonstrating the successful removal of contaminants.

PCR contamination troubleshooting and potential solutions

Gel images depicting the quality control steps for PCR2-NGS amplicons.

(A) Analysis of a gel image from a Agilent 2100 Bioanalyzer high sensitivity DNA Chip run reveals the presence of contaminants, denoted by asterisks.

(B) Following purification with an AMPure XP PCR purification kit, the same samples were re-run and analyzed, demonstrating the successful removal of contaminants.

Materials availability

Plasmids and cell lines generated in this study are available upon written request to the lead contact. Please consult the list of unique reagents in the key resources table. All reagents listed here can be found in the main article Ordoñez et al., 2021.1

Data and code availability

The raw and processed high-throughput sequencing data from the CRISPR screen reported in this study are available at NCBI’s Gene Expression Omnibus (GEO, accession number: GSE158574). The processed data includes the full gene-ranking list of top hits.
